We highlight that this is again a basic point where
triangulated
categories would not suffice. That is, one really needs the complexes, and not
simply their cohomology, due to the non-exactness of inverse limits.

Colimits of diagrams of dg-categories are then characterized by the identity

\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{DGCat}_{\operatorname{cont}}}(\varinjlim\mathscr{C}_{\alpha},-)\simeq\varprojlim\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{DGCat}_{\operatorname{cont}}}(\mathscr{C}_{\alpha},-)

and, relatedly, limits of dg-categories satisfy the identity

\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{DGCat}_{\operatorname{cont}}}(-,\varprojlim\mathscr{C}_{\beta})\simeq\varprojlim\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{DGCat}_{\operatorname{cont}}}(-,\mathscr{C}_{\beta}).

It is not easy to describe the objects or morphisms in a general colimit of
dg-categories (and indeed, one in general should only expect straightforward
descriptions for limits, as they are characterized by a mapping in
property). However, to orient the reader, let us describe an important example
that admits an explicit description.

Example 4.2.12
.

Recall that an object
c
of a dg-category
\mathscr{C}

is said to be
compact
if

\operatorname{Hom}(c,-)

commutes with colimits. These should be thought of
as
small objects, e.g., in
\operatorname{Vect}
an object is compact if and only if it is
equivalent to a bounded complex of finite dimensional vector spaces. Relatedly,

\mathscr{C}
is compactly generated if every object can be written as a colimit of
compact objects.

Given a filtered diagram of compactly generated categories
\mathscr{C}_{\alpha}
, where
each 1-morphism
\mathscr{C}_{\alpha}\rightarrow\mathscr{C}_{\alpha^{\prime}}
sends compact objects to
compact objects, the colimit

\varinjlim\mathscr{C}_{\alpha}

admits the following description. It is again compactly generated by insertions
of compact objects from each
\mathscr{C}_{\alpha}
, and homomorphisms between such
objects are the filtered colimit of the homomorphisms at each step in the
colimit.

4.2.13.

A very useful feature of
\operatorname{DGCat}_{\operatorname{cont}}
is that one can tensor together
cocomplete dg-categories

\mathscr{C}\text{ and }\mathscr{D}\rightsquigarrow\mathscr{C}\otimes\mathscr{D}.

More carefully,
\operatorname{DGCat}_{\operatorname{cont}}
is a symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category with respect
to the Lurie tensor product.

In many practical examples, the following hands-on description of the tensor
product is useful.

Example 4.2.14
.

Suppose that
\mathscr{C}
and
\mathscr{D}
are compactly generated, say by sets of
objects
c_{\alpha}
and
d_{\beta}
. Then the tensor product is compactly
generated by the set of simple tensors
c_{\alpha}\otimes d_{\beta}
, with
homomorphisms given by the Künneth formula, i.e.,

\operatorname{Hom}(c_{\alpha}\otimes d_{\beta},c_{\alpha^{\prime}}\otimes d_{\beta^{\prime}})\simeq\operatorname{Hom}(c_{\alpha},c_{\alpha^{\prime}})\otimes\operatorname{Hom}(d_{\beta},d_{\beta^{\prime}}).

Here are some special cases of the above example, which hopefully convey to the
reader the sense in which this tensor product aligns with their intuition from
the functions-sheaves correspondence, cf. Section
2.2.4
.

Example 4.2.15
.

Given algebraic varieties
X
and
Y
, one has a canonical
equivalence of the categories of quasicoherent sheaves

\operatorname{QCoh}(X)\otimes\operatorname{QCoh}(Y)\simeq\operatorname{QCoh}(X\times Y).

Explicitly, given an object
\mathscr{M}
of
\operatorname{QCoh}(X)
and
\mathscr{N}
of

\operatorname{QCoh}(Y)
, the simple tensor
\mathscr{M}\otimes\mathscr{N}
of the left hand side is sent to the
external product object

\mathscr{M}\boxtimes\mathscr{N}\simeq\pi_{X}^{*}\mathscr{M}\overset{}{\otimes}\pi_{Y}^{*}\mathscr{N}.

Example 4.2.16
.

Let
X
and
Y
be as in the preceding example. One also has a
canonical equivalence of the categories of D-modules

\operatorname{D-mod}(X)\otimes\operatorname{D-mod}(Y)\simeq\operatorname{D-mod}(X\times Y).

Again, given an object
\mathscr{M}
of
\operatorname{D-mod}(X)
and an object
\mathscr{N}
of

\operatorname{D-mod}(Y)
, the simple tensor
\mathscr{M}\otimes\mathscr{N}
of the left hand side is sent to the
external product object

\mathscr{M}\boxtimes\mathscr{N}\simeq\pi_{X}^{!}\mathscr{N}\overset{!}{\otimes}\pi_{Y}^{!}\mathscr{M}.

Example 4.2.17
.

Given
k
-algebras
A
and
B
, one has a canonical equivalence

A\operatorname{-mod}\otimes\hskip 0.56905ptB\operatorname{-mod}\simeq(A\underset{k}{\otimes}B)\operatorname{-mod}.

Note that this generalizes the affine cases of the previous two examples.

Let us mention that the monoidal structure is unital, with unit the category of
vector spaces
\operatorname{Vect}
. In particular, for any dg-category
\mathscr{C}
, one has a
canonical equivalence

\operatorname{Vect}\otimes\hskip 1.99168pt\mathscr{C}\simeq\mathscr{C}.

The reader may wish to check this directly in the case
\mathscr{C}
is compactly
generated.

4.2.18.

Given dg-categories
\mathscr{C}
and
\mathscr{D}
, the category
\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{DGCat}_{\operatorname{cont}}}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D})
of Section
4.2.7
is an internal hom object with respect to
the monoidal structure on
\operatorname{DGCat}_{\operatorname{cont}}
.

A technical point is that this is not
the same as the category of morphisms between
\mathscr{C}
and
\mathscr{D}
, viewed as objects of

\operatorname{DGCat}_{\operatorname{cont}}
. Instead, the
category of morphisms is the truncation of

\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{DGCat}_{\operatorname{cont}}}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D})
to an
\infty
-groupoid, i.e., one discards the non-invertible
natural
transformations. It is expected that one has a canonical enhancement of

\operatorname{DGCat}_{\operatorname{cont}}
to a
symmetric monoidal
(\infty,2)
-category, where the homomorphisms are indeed

\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{DGCat}_{\operatorname{cont}}}(-,-)
, but to our knowledge this is not yet available in the
literature.

4.2.19.

Using the monoidal structure, one can make sense of algebra
objects of
\operatorname{DGCat}_{\operatorname{cont}}
, i.e., monoidal dg-categories. Informally, this
consists of a dg-category
\mathscr{A}
with a multiplication

\mathscr{A}\otimes\mathscr{A}\rightarrow\mathscr{A},

which is associative up to coherent homotopy. We emphasize the coherent homotopy
is further structure and is part of the full data of a monoidal dg-category.

Here are some basic examples of monoidal dg-categories.

Example 4.2.20
.

For an algebraic variety
X
,
\operatorname{QCoh}(X)
is a
monoidal dg-category
under
*
-tensor product, and
\operatorname{D-mod}(X)
is a monoidal dg-category under

!
-tensor product.

Given a map
f:X_{1}\rightarrow X_{2}
, the corresponding pullback functors

f^{*}:\operatorname{QCoh}(X_{2})\rightarrow\operatorname{QCoh}(X_{1})\quad\text{and}\quad f^{!}:\operatorname{D-mod}(X_{2})\rightarrow\operatorname{D-mod}(X_{1})

carry a canonical datum of monoidality.

Example 4.2.21
.

Suppose a variety
Y
is an algebra object in the category of
varieties. That is, it is equipped with a map

\mu:Y\times Y\rightarrow Y,

which is associative in the evident sense. Then
\operatorname{QCoh}(Y)
and
\operatorname{D-mod}(Y)
both
acquire ‘convolution’ monoidal structures, given by
*
-pushforward along

\mu
.

Given a map
f:Y_{1}\rightarrow Y_{2}
of algebras, the associated pushforward
maps

f_{*}:\operatorname{QCoh}(Y_{1})\rightarrow\operatorname{QCoh}(Y_{2})\quad\text{and}\quad f_{*}:\operatorname{D-mod}(Y_{1})\rightarrow\operatorname{D-mod}(Y_{2})

carry a canonical datum of monoidality.

As our primary case of interest, we take
Y=K
to be an algebraic
group, with
\mu
its multiplication, and thereby obtain the group algebra

\operatorname{D-mod}(K)
we met previously (or rather, its canonical dg-enhancement).

Similarly, given a homomorphism
\phi:K_{1}\rightarrow K_{2}
of groups, one
obtains a homomorphism of group algebras

\phi_{*}:\operatorname{D-mod}(K_{1})\rightarrow\operatorname{D-mod}(K_{2}).

Remark 4.2.22
.

One can replace in the two preceding paragraphs
\operatorname{D-mod}(K)
with
\operatorname{QCoh}(K)
to obtain another monoidal dg-category under convolution. Modules for
\operatorname{D-mod}(K)
are sometimes called strong categorical representations and modules for
\operatorname{QCoh}(K)
weak categorical representations.

It may be clarifying to remark that, given any monoidal functor from varieties to a monoidal
\infty
-category
(\mathscr{S},\otimes)
, we obtain a similar convolution algebra linearizing
K
. In particular, one can speak of further higher categorical versions of
\operatorname{Fun}(K(\mathbb{F}_{q}))
, e.g., by replacing sheaves with sheaves of categories on
K
.

4.2.23.

Given a monoidal dg-category
\mathscr{A}
, one has associated

\infty
-categories of its left and right modules, which we denote by

\mathscr{A}\operatorname{-mod}\quad\text{and}\quad\operatorname{mod-}\mathscr{A},

respectively. Somewhat informally, a left module consists of a cocomplete
dg-category
\mathscr{M}
equipped with an action map

\mathscr{A}\otimes\mathscr{M}\rightarrow\mathscr{M},

which is ‘associative’ up to coherent homotopy and similarly for a right
module. Again, the coherent homotopy is part of the data of specifying a
module.

Given two left modules
\mathscr{M}_{1}
and
\mathscr{M}_{2}
, one has the
dg-category of

\mathscr{A}
-equivariant functors

\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathscr{A}\operatorname{-mod}}(\mathscr{M}_{1},\mathscr{M}_{2})

(and similarly for right modules over
\mathscr{A}
). Explicitly this may be calculated via a bar complex as the limit of the
semi-cosimplicial diagram of dg-categories

(4.3)

\textstyle{\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{DGCat}_{\operatorname{cont}}}(\mathscr{M}_{1},\mathscr{M}_{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\hskip 1.42262pt\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{DGCat}_{\operatorname{cont}}}(\mathscr{A}\otimes\mathscr{M}_{1},\mathscr{M}_{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\hskip 1.42262pt\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{DGCat}_{\operatorname{cont}}}(\mathscr{A}^{\otimes 2}\otimes\mathscr{M}_{1},\mathscr{M}_{2})\hskip 1.42262pt\cdots\hskip 1.42262pt,}

where the arrows are induced in the standard pattern of bar resolutions by
the action maps of
\mathscr{A}
on
\mathscr{M}_{1}
and
\mathscr{M}_{2}
and the multiplication map

\mathscr{A}^{\otimes 2}\rightarrow\mathscr{A}
.

In addition, given a right module
\mathscr{M}
and a left module
\mathscr{N}
, one can form
their tensor product

\mathscr{M}\underset{\mathscr{A}}{\otimes}\mathscr{N}.

As for homomorphisms, this may be computed via a bar resolution as the colimit of
the semisimplicial diagram of cocomplete dg-categories

(4.4)

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 34.6141pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-34.6141pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\cdots\hskip 1.42262pt\mathscr{M}\otimes\mathscr{A}^{\otimes 2}\otimes N\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 58.6141pt\raise-3.44444pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 58.6141pt\raise 3.44444pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 58.6141pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 58.6141pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\hskip 1.42262pt\mathscr{M}\otimes\mathscr{A}\otimes\mathscr{N}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 129.75903pt\raise-1.72218pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 129.75903pt\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 129.75903pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\hskip 1.42262pt\mathscr{M}\otimes\mathscr{N}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

Let us now give a few basic examples.

Example 4.2.24
.

For any left
\mathscr{A}
-module
\mathscr{M}
, there are tautological equivalences

\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathscr{A}\operatorname{-mod}}(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{M})\simeq\mathscr{M}\simeq\mathscr{A}\underset{\mathscr{A}}{\otimes}\mathscr{M}

and similarly for right modules.

Example 4.2.25
.

Given maps of varieties
X\rightarrow Z\leftarrow Y
, as in
Example
4.2.20
, we obtain canonical actions of
\operatorname{QCoh}(Z)
on

\operatorname{QCoh}(X)
and
\operatorname{QCoh}(Y)
. With this, their tensor product is given by

\operatorname{QCoh}(X)\underset{\operatorname{QCoh}(Z)}{\otimes}\operatorname{QCoh}(Y)\simeq\operatorname{QCoh}(X\underset{Z}{\times}Y),

where the above fibre product is derived, i.e., one obtains a
derived scheme.
9
9

9

Plainly, this is glued from the derived rings
\mathscr{O}_{X}\underset{\mathscr{O}_{Z}}{\overset{L}{\otimes}}\mathscr{O}_{Y}
affine by affine, exactly as in
the case of the usual fibre product. One thinks of the negative cohomology groups of the structure sheaf as adding ‘nilpotent fluff’ to the variety, exactly like the perhaps more familiar the non-reducedness of a general scheme. All derived schemes we will meet in this survey arise in this way.

Concretely, the equivalence sends a simple tensor
\mathscr{M}\otimes\mathscr{N}
to their
external product, restricted to
X\underset{Z}{\times}Y
.

Similarly, their homomorphisms are again given by

\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{QCoh}(Z)}(\operatorname{QCoh}(X),\operatorname{QCoh}(Y))\simeq\operatorname{QCoh}(X\underset{Z}{\times}Y).

Explicitly, one associates to a quasicoherent sheaf
\mathscr{S}
on the fibre product
the integral transform

\operatorname{QCoh}(X)\xrightarrow{\pi_{X}^{*}}\operatorname{QCoh}(X\underset{Z}{\times}Y)\xrightarrow{-\overset{*}{\otimes}\mathscr{S}}\operatorname{QCoh}(X\underset{Z}{\times}Y)\xrightarrow{\pi_{Z,*}}\operatorname{QCoh}(Y).

The same assertions hold, mutatis mutandis, for D-modules. That is, we have
equivalences

\displaystyle\operatorname{D-mod}(X)\underset{\operatorname{D-mod}(Z)}{\otimes}\operatorname{D-mod}(Y)

\displaystyle\simeq\operatorname{D-mod}(X\overset{\operatorname{cl}}{{\underset{Z}{\times}}}Y)\text{ and}

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{D-mod}(Z)}(\operatorname{D-mod}(X),\operatorname{D-mod}(Y))

\displaystyle\simeq\operatorname{D-mod}(X\overset{\operatorname{cl}}{{\underset{Z}{\times}}}Y),

again given by external tensor product and integral transform. Here the superscript ‘
\operatorname{cl}
’ denotes the classical, i.e., non-derived, fibre product. Note however that when the derived fibre product has only finitely many nonzero cohomologies in its structure sheaf, e.g., if
Z
is smooth, due to the invariance of categories of D-modules under
nil-thickenings it is
unimportant whether one takes the derived or classical fiber product of
varieties.

As with other sheaf-theoretic statements we have met, these identities are
analogues
of the
following identity for functions. Given a finite set
S
, consider
\operatorname{Fun}(S)
as
an algebra
under
pointwise multiplication. Given finite sets
X\rightarrow Z\leftarrow Y
, one then has canonical isomorphisms of vector spaces

\operatorname{Fun}(X)\underset{\operatorname{Fun}(Z)}{\otimes}\operatorname{Fun}(Y)\simeq\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{Fun}(Z)}(\operatorname{Fun}(X),\operatorname{Fun}(Y))\simeq\operatorname{Fun}(X\underset{Z}{\times}Y).

4.3.
Basics of categorical representation theory

4.3.1.

With these generalities in hand, let us describe some of the
basic operations in categorical representation theory and thereby recover Theorems
3.4.10
and
3.4.12
encountered in Section
3
.

Recall that, for an algebraic group
K
, we have its associated
\infty
-category of
categorical representations

\operatorname{D-mod}(K)\operatorname{-mod}.

As elsewhere in representation theory, inversion on
K
induces an isomorphism
between
\operatorname{D-mod}(K)
and its reverse monoidal dg-category, and in particular one
may freely pass between left and right modules.

4.3.2.

A basic family of representations may be constructed
geometrically as follows.

Given a variety
X
with an action of
K
, one obtains a canonical action of

\operatorname{D-mod}(K)
on
\operatorname{D-mod}(X)
. Explicitly, the underlying binary product is given by

\operatorname{D-mod}(K)\otimes\operatorname{D-mod}(X)\simeq\operatorname{D-mod}(K\times X)\xrightarrow{a_{*}}\operatorname{D-mod}(X),

exactly as in the function-theoretic case of Section
2.3.2
. Given a
map
f:X\rightarrow X^{\prime}
of
K
-varieties, the functors of
*
-pushforward and

!
-pullback along
f
carry canonical data of
K
-equivariance.

4.3.3.

In particular, if we take
X=\operatorname{pt}
, we obtain the trivial
representation of
K
on

\operatorname{Vect}\simeq\operatorname{D-mod}(\operatorname{pt}).

Concretely, if we write
\pi:K\rightarrow\operatorname{pt}
for the projection, the
action map takes the form

\operatorname{D-mod}(K)\otimes\operatorname{Vect}\rightarrow\operatorname{Vect},\quad\quad\mathscr{M}\otimes V\mapsto\pi_{*}(\mathscr{M})\underset{k}{\otimes}V.

For any representation
\mathscr{C}
of
K
, we may accordingly form its invariants and
coinvariants

\mathscr{C}^{K}:=\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{D-mod}(K)\operatorname{-mod}}(\operatorname{Vect},\mathscr{C})\quad\text{and}\quad\mathscr{C}_{K}:=\operatorname{Vect}\underset{\operatorname{D-mod}(K)}{\otimes}\mathscr{C}.

These are canonically identified, as in the case of usual representations of
finite groups, as follows.

By the smoothness of
K
, we have a
\operatorname{D-mod}(K)
equivariant adjunction

\pi^{!}[-2\dim K]:\operatorname{D-mod}(K)\rightleftarrows\operatorname{Vect}:\pi_{*},

which upon applying
\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{D-mod}(K)\operatorname{-mod}}(-,\mathscr{C})
induces an adjunction

\operatorname{Oblv}:\mathscr{C}^{K}\rightleftarrows\mathscr{C}:\operatorname{Av}_{*}.

As the notation suggests, one thinks of
\operatorname{Oblv}
as a forgetful functor and

\operatorname{Av}_{*}
as an averaging functor. For formal reasons, the averaging functor
factors through a map out of the coinvariants

\operatorname{Av}_{*}:\mathscr{C}_{K}\rightarrow\mathscr{C}^{K}.

By a very useful theorem of Beraldo and Gaitsgory
[
Ber17a
]
,
[
Gai20
]
, this is an equivalence.

Let us give some basic examples of (co)invariants.

Example 4.3.4
.

Given
\operatorname{D-mod}(K)
representations
\mathscr{C}
and
\mathscr{D}
, one has a canonical
equivalence

\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{D-mod}(K)\operatorname{-mod}}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D})\simeq\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{DGCat}_{\operatorname{cont}}}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D})^{K},

where the invariants are taken with respect to the diagonal action of

\operatorname{D-mod}(K)
on the space of maps.

Example 4.3.5
.

For a
K
-variety
X
, the
K
-equivariant
objects in
\operatorname{D-mod}(X)

canonically identify with the category of D-modules on the quotient stack

X/K
, i.e.,

\operatorname{D-mod}(X)^{K}\simeq\operatorname{D-mod}(X/K).

Plainly, D-modules satisfy smooth descent and the diagram (
4.3
)
simply is the ‘
\check{\operatorname{C}}
ech complex’ for calculating D-modules on
X/K
via the cover

X\rightarrow X/K
.

Example 4.3.6
.

Under the natural action of
\operatorname{D-mod}(K)
on its category of Lie
algebra representations
\mathfrak{k}\operatorname{-mod}
, the invariants identify with the
algebraic representations of the group itself, i.e.,

\mathfrak{k}\operatorname{-mod}^{K}\simeq K\operatorname{-mod}.

More generally, given a homomorphism
K\rightarrow K^{\prime}
, the category

\mathfrak{k}^{\prime}\operatorname{-mod}^{K}

canonically identifies with the category of Harish-Chandra modules for the pair
(\mathfrak{k}^{\prime},K)
.

Example 4.3.7
.

Here is another class of examples, which in particular gives an alternative perspective on the action on
\mathfrak{k}\operatorname{-mod}
. Suppose
A
is a
k
-algebra equipped with an action of
K
by automorphisms. In this case, one obtains an action of
\operatorname{QCoh}(K)
on
A\operatorname{-mod}
.

Upon differentiation at the identity, the action of
K
on
A
yields an action
\alpha
of
\mathfrak{k}
on
A
by derivations. Suppose one is further given a trivialization of this action, i.e., a homomorphism of Lie algebras

\tau:\mathfrak{k}\rightarrow A,

such that
\alpha
agrees with the adjoint action via
\tau
. In this case, one obtains an action of
\operatorname{D-mod}(K)
on
A\operatorname{-mod}
.

Remark 4.3.8
.

Let us record here a basic point of departure from the case of usual
representations. Namely, the operation of taking invariants is not
idempotent, i.e., the tautological map

\operatorname{Oblv}_{\mathscr{C}^{K}}:(\mathscr{C}^{K})^{K}\rightarrow\mathscr{C}^{K}

is typically not an isomorphism. Instead, one has a canonical equivalence

(\mathscr{C}^{K})^{K}\simeq(\mathscr{C}^{K})\otimes\operatorname{D-mod}(\operatorname{pt/K}).

This happens because, unless
K
is
contractible, the forgetful map
\operatorname{Oblv}_{\mathscr{D}}:\mathscr{D}^{K}\rightarrow\mathscr{D}
for any (nonzero) representation
\mathscr{D}

will not be
fully faithful, i.e., Exts in
\mathscr{D}^{K}
and
\mathscr{D}
do not agree. Indeed, in the universal case of
\mathscr{D}=\operatorname{D-mod}(K)
, one has

\operatorname{D-mod}(K)^{K}\simeq\operatorname{D-mod}(K/K)\simeq\operatorname{Vect},

with generator the constant sheaf. In particular its self Exts in
\operatorname{D-mod}(K)
are
\pi_{*}(\underline{k})
, i.e., the cohomology of
K
, while its self Exts in
\operatorname{Vect}
are trivial.

In general, the
composition

\mathscr{D}^{K}\xrightarrow{\operatorname{Oblv}_{\mathscr{D}}}\mathscr{D}\xrightarrow{\operatorname{Av}_{*}}\mathscr{D}^{K}\xrightarrow{\operatorname{Oblv}_{\mathscr{D}}}\mathscr{D}

canonically identifies with
\operatorname{Oblv}_{\mathscr{D}}\otimes\pi_{*}(\underline{k})
, i.e.,
tensors the underlying object of a
K
-equivariant object with the cohomology
of
K
. The reader may wish to compare this, via the functions-sheaves correspondence,
to the factor of the cardinality of a finite group that arises in the
discussion of averaging for usual representations.

Note, however, that if
K
is unipotent then
\operatorname{Oblv}
is fully
faithful, as
K
is contractible and hence
\pi_{*}(\underline{k})\simeq k
.

4.3.9.

Having discussed the functor of invariants, let us indicate how one obtains Theorems
3.4.10
and
3.4.12
, beginning with the latter.

Given a map of algebraic groups
H\rightarrow K
, one has
induction and restriction functors

\operatorname{ind}_{H}^{K}:\operatorname{D-mod}(H)\operatorname{-mod}\rightarrow\operatorname{D-mod}(K)\operatorname{-mod},

\operatorname{res}_{K}^{H}:\operatorname{D-mod}(K)\operatorname{-mod}\rightarrow\operatorname{D-mod}(H)\operatorname{-mod}.

Plainly,
\operatorname{res}_{K}^{H}
restricts the action of
\operatorname{D-mod}(K)
on a dg-category

\mathscr{C}
along the monoidal functor

\operatorname{D-mod}(H)\rightarrow\operatorname{D-mod}(K),

and
\operatorname{ind}_{H}^{K}
takes a dg-category
\mathscr{D}
with an action of
\operatorname{D-mod}(H)
and
tensors it up, i.e.,

\mathscr{D}\mapsto\operatorname{D-mod}(K)\underset{\operatorname{D-mod}(H)}{\otimes}\mathscr{D}.

As with Frobenius reciprocity for finite groups, induction and restriction are
canonically both left and right adjoint to one another.

In particular, one obtains a functor of
H
-invariants on

\operatorname{D-mod}(K)
representations, i.e.,

\displaystyle\big(\operatorname{Res}_{K}^{H}(-)\big)^{H}

\displaystyle\simeq\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{D-mod}(H)\operatorname{-mod}}(\operatorname{Vect},\operatorname{res}_{K}^{H}-).

By adjunction we may rewrite this as

\displaystyle\simeq\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{D-mod}(K)\operatorname{-mod}}(\operatorname{ind}_{H}^{K}\operatorname{Vect},-)

\displaystyle\simeq\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{D-mod}(K)\operatorname{-mod}}(\operatorname{D-mod}(K)\underset{\operatorname{D-mod}(H)}{\otimes}\operatorname{Vect},-)

By the identification of
H
invariants and coinvariants and
Example
4.3.5
, we may recognize this as

\displaystyle\simeq\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{D-mod}(K)\operatorname{-mod}}(\operatorname{D-mod}(K/H),-).

The upshot is that the
H
-invariants of any
\operatorname{D-mod}(K)
representation carry, by
precomposition, a
canonical action of

\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{D-mod}(K)\operatorname{-mod}}(\operatorname{D-mod}(K/H),\operatorname{D-mod}(K/H))\simeq\operatorname{D-mod}(H\backslash K/H),

i.e., the Hecke algebra action of Beilinson–Drinfeld we encountered at the end
of Section

3
.

4.3.10.

Let us finally sketch, for a smooth
K
-variety
X
, how one obtains the
\operatorname{D-mod}(K)
equivariant adjunction

\operatorname{Loc}:\mathfrak{k}\operatorname{-mod}\rightleftarrows\operatorname{D-mod}(X):\Gamma(X,-).

To begin with, induction from
\mathscr{O}_{K}
-modules to
\mathscr{D}_{K}
-modules yields a monoidal functor

\operatorname{QCoh}(K)\rightarrow\operatorname{D-mod}(K).

Similarly, one has a canonical action of
\operatorname{QCoh}(K)
on
\operatorname{D-mod}(X)
such that the forgetful functor
\operatorname{D-mod}(X)\rightarrow\operatorname{QCoh}(X)
carries a canonical datum of
\operatorname{QCoh}(K)
-equivariance. In particular, the functor of global sections, i.e., the composition

\operatorname{D-mod}(X)\rightarrow\operatorname{QCoh}(X)\xrightarrow{\pi_{*}}\operatorname{QCoh}(\operatorname{pt})\simeq\operatorname{Vect},

carries a canonical datum of
\operatorname{QCoh}(K)
-equivariance.

However, for any
\operatorname{D-mod}(K)
representation
\mathscr{C}
, one has by adjunction

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{QCoh}(K)\operatorname{-mod}}(\operatorname{Res}\mathscr{C},\operatorname{Vect})

\displaystyle\simeq\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{D-mod}(K)\operatorname{-mod}}(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{QCoh}(K)\operatorname{-mod}}(\operatorname{D-mod}(K),\operatorname{Vect}))

\displaystyle\simeq\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{D-mod}(K)\operatorname{-mod}}(\mathscr{C},\mathfrak{k}\operatorname{-mod}),

where for the last equivalence one uses the identification of
\mathfrak{k}\operatorname{-mod}
and weakly
K
-equivariant D-modules on
K
, cf. Section
3.4.8
. This yields the desired
\operatorname{D-mod}(K)
equivariance of the global sections functor. For localization, one can use the nice fact, due to Gaitsgory
[
Gai20
]
, that any adjoint of a
\operatorname{D-mod}(K)\operatorname{-mod}
-equivariant functor inherits a compatible datum of equivariance.

4.4.
Complements I - character sheaves

4.4.1.

In this section, we began by sketching some preliminary
generalities on dg-categories. We then discussed some of the basic definitions and
constructions in categorical representation theory, sufficient to recover some
results previewed in Section
3
.

In the remainder of this section, we would like to survey a few more basic
results and constructions. However, the reader may wish to skip directly to
Section
5
.

4.4.2.

Character sheaves are (certain) adjoint equivariant D-modules
on groups. Lusztig initiated their study, particularly in the reductive case, in view of their close ties to irreducible characters of
finite groups of Lie type
[
Lus85a
,
Lus85b
,
Lus86a
,
Lus86b
]
. This is a particularly rich and nontrivial example of the
functions-sheaves correspondence.

Of course, the characters of a finite group are not simply adjoint equivariant
functions on the group, but rather arise as traces from representations. One
can ask whether character sheaves have such an interpretation. Indeed they
do, and we turn to this next.

4.4.3.
Duality

Recall that to define the character of a
representation of a finite group, one needs the representation to be finite
dimensional so that one may safely take traces. The analogue of being ‘small enough’ to take traces in the categorical setting is as follows.

Using the monoidal structure on
\operatorname{DGCat}_{\operatorname{cont}}
one can speak
of dualizable dg-categories. Under the analogy between
\operatorname{DGCat}_{\operatorname{cont}}
and
\operatorname{Vect}
,
these correspond to (bounded complexes of) finite dimensional vector spaces,
and indeed a dg-category
\mathscr{C}
is dualizable if and only if the natural map

\mathscr{C}\rightarrow\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{DGCat}_{\operatorname{cont}}}(\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{DGCat}_{\operatorname{cont}}}(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{Vect}),\operatorname{Vect})

is an equivalence.

For a dualizable dg-category, one has a canonical
equivalence
\mathscr{C}^{\vee}\simeq\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{DGCat}_{\operatorname{cont}}}(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{Vect})
, i.e., its dual in the monoidal sense is simply its continuous
dg-modules. For any
\mathscr{S}
the natural map

\mathscr{S}\otimes\mathscr{C}^{\vee}\rightarrow\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{DGCat}_{\operatorname{cont}}}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{S})

is an equivalence.
10
10

10

Replacing
\mathscr{C}^{\vee}
with
\operatorname{Hom}(\mathscr{C},\operatorname{Vect})
, this
is yet another characterization of dualizable dg-categories.
More generally,
there are equivalences

\operatorname{Hom}(\mathscr{S}\otimes\mathscr{C},\mathscr{S}^{\prime})\simeq\operatorname{Hom}(\mathscr{S},\mathscr{C}^{\vee}\otimes\mathscr{S}^{\prime})

for any dg-categories
\mathscr{S}
and
\mathscr{S}^{\prime}
.

Let
\mathscr{A}
be a monoidal dg-category. By functoriality, if
\mathscr{C}
is a left
\mathscr{A}
-module, then
\mathscr{C}^{\vee}
is a right
\mathscr{A}
-module. In particular, for a
dualizable
\operatorname{D-mod}(K)
representation
\mathscr{C}
, its dual acquires a canonical action of

\operatorname{D-mod}(K)
, i.e., one may form the contragredient representation.

Before we discuss how to define the character sheaf of a dualizable
\operatorname{D-mod}(K)
representation, we would like to discuss some basic examples of dualizable categories and modules. First of
all, the following general fact ensures that most dg-categories one encounters in practice are
dualizable.

Example 4.4.4
.

If a category
\mathscr{C}
is compactly generated, then it is dualizable.
Moreover, its dual is compactly generated, and one has a canonical equivalence
of non-cocomplete dg-categories between the compact objects of
\mathscr{C}
and the
opposite of the compact objects of
\mathscr{C}^{\vee}
.

Here are some useful special cases of the previous example.

Example 4.4.5
.

Given a
k
-algebra
A
, its category of left-modules
A\operatorname{-mod}
is
dualizable with dual the category of right modules
\operatorname{mod-}A
. Explicitly,
the
evaluation map

\operatorname{mod-}A\otimes A\operatorname{-mod}\rightarrow\operatorname{Vect}

sends a right module
M
and left module
N
to their tensor product
M\underset{A}{\otimes}N
.

Example 4.4.6
.

Given a variety
X
, its category of D-modules is made canonically
self-dual by the operation of Verdier duality on compact D-modules. Explicitly, the
resulting evaluation map is given by the composition

\operatorname{D-mod}(X)\otimes\operatorname{D-mod}(X)\simeq\operatorname{D-mod}(X\times X)\xrightarrow{\Delta^{!}}\operatorname{D-mod}(X)\xrightarrow{\pi_{*}}\operatorname{Vect},

where, exactly as in Section
2.3.3
, we denote by
\Delta:X\rightarrow X\times X
the diagonal map and
\pi:X\rightarrow\operatorname{pt}

the projection to the
point.

If
X
is a
K
-variety, this pairing is canonically

\operatorname{D-mod}(K)
equivariant, i.e., identifies
\operatorname{D-mod}(X)
with its contragredient
representation. Similar statements hold, mutatis mutandis, for
\operatorname{QCoh}(X)
, viewed as a
\operatorname{QCoh}(K)
representation.

4.4.7.
Character sheaves

We are now ready to explain how to take the character sheaf of a representation. An action of
\operatorname{D-mod}(K)
on a category
\mathscr{C}

is the same as a monoidal functor

\operatorname{D-mod}(K)\xrightarrow{\rho}\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{DGCat}_{\operatorname{cont}}}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{C}).

If
\mathscr{C}
is dualizable, then its endomorphisms
\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{DGCat}_{\operatorname{cont}}}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{C})
are again dualizable by the identification
\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{DGCat}_{\operatorname{cont}}}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{C})\simeq\mathscr{C}^{\vee}\otimes\mathscr{C}
. We may therefore dualize the action
map to obtain a
\operatorname{D-mod}(K\times K)
-equivariant functor of matrix coefficients

\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{DGCat}_{\operatorname{cont}}}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{C})\simeq\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{DGCat}_{\operatorname{cont}}}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{C})^{\vee}\xrightarrow{\rho^{\vee}}\operatorname{D-mod}(K)^{\vee}\simeq\operatorname{D-mod}(K).

In particular, by passing to diagonal
K
-invariants, we obtain a functor

(4.5)

\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{D-mod}(K)}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{C})\rightarrow\operatorname{D-mod}(K/K).

Applying this to the identity endomorphism
\text{id}_{\mathscr{C}}
, we obtain an adjoint
equivariant D-module
\chi_{\mathscr{C}}
on
K
,
namely the
character sheaf
of
\mathscr{C}
.

4.4.8.

Here is a more hands-on description of
\chi_{\mathscr{C}}
. Given a
dualizable category
\mathscr{C}
and an endofunctor
f:\mathscr{C}\rightarrow\mathscr{C}
, one can
take its trace, which is a complex of vector spaces. Exactly as in linear
algebra, this is computed via the composition

\operatorname{Vect}\xrightarrow{\text{id}_{\mathscr{C}}}\mathscr{C}\otimes\mathscr{C}^{\vee}\xrightarrow{f\otimes\operatorname{id}_{\mathscr{C}^{\vee}}}\mathscr{C}\otimes\mathscr{C}^{\vee}\xrightarrow{\operatorname{ev}}\operatorname{Vect}.

That is, the composite endofunctor of
\operatorname{Vect}
is given by tensoring by a vector space, which is by
definition
\operatorname{tr}(f,\mathscr{C})
. We remark that, when
f
is simply the identity
endomorphism of
\mathscr{C}
, the resulting complex is known as the Hochschild
homology of
\mathscr{C}
.

Having understood how to take traces in the present categorical context, we can now describe the character sheaf fairly explicitly. Namely, its stalks store the traces of the
corresponding automorphisms of
\mathscr{C}
, exactly as in usual representation
theory. That is, for any closed point
g:\operatorname{pt}\rightarrow K
, one has a
canonical isomorphism

g^{!}(\chi_{\mathscr{C}})\simeq\operatorname{tr}(\delta_{g}\star-,\mathscr{C}).

4.4.9.

Let us mention a few features and examples.

Example 4.4.10
.

A
\operatorname{D-mod}(K)
equivariant functor between dualizable modules

\mathscr{C}\rightarrow\mathscr{D}

need not induce a map between their character sheaves. However, if it admits
a continuous right adjoint, we do obtain a suitably functorial map
\chi_{\mathscr{C}}\rightarrow\chi_{\mathscr{D}}
.

Let us remark that, in practical situations,
\mathscr{C}
is compactly generated,
and the condition of being right adjointable is equivalent to sending
compact objects to compact objects, i.e., nothing ‘small’ becoming ‘big’.

Example 4.4.11
.

The induction and restriction functors on categorical
representations preserve the property of dualizability. On characters, they
reproduce the usual functors of induction and restriction for character
sheaves.

Example 4.4.12
.

If
X
is a
K
-variety, its character sheaf may be described as
follows. Consider the
closed subscheme
\operatorname{Stab}

of
K\times X
, whose fibre over a point of
X
is its stabilizer. That is,

\operatorname{Stab}
is the
equalizer of the action and
projection maps to
X
. Then under the tautological
G
-equivariant map

\operatorname{Stab}\rightarrow G,

the pushforward of the dualizing sheaf is
\chi_{\operatorname{D-mod}(X)}
. In particular, by
proper base change, the
trace of any element of
K
on
\operatorname{D-mod}(X)
is the Borel–Moore homology of its
fixed points. The reader may wish to compare this with the analogous assertion for finite groups and
sets, where the trace of an element of the group recovers the number of fixed points.

Example 4.4.13
.

If
K=G
is a reductive group, and
\mathscr{C}=\operatorname{D-mod}(\operatorname{Fl}_{G})
, then by the
preceding example its
character sheaf is the Grothendieck–Springer sheaf on
G/G
. More generally,
the functor of matrix coefficients for equivariant endomorphisms, cf. Equation
(
4.5
), takes the form

\operatorname{D-mod}(B\backslash G/B)\rightarrow\operatorname{D-mod}(G/G).

This recovers the Harish-Chandra, or horocycle, transform, i.e., push-pull along
the correspondence

G/G\leftarrow G/B\rightarrow B\backslash G/B,

where
the left and middle terms are quotients with respect to the adjoint action. The
latter was the point of departure for Lusztig’s study of character sheaves.

Here are two related remarks.

Remark 4.4.14
.

In the present discussion, we treated character sheaves as a
direct construction, which takes as input dualizable representations and
outputs adjoint equivariant D-modules.

An alternative perspective is to interpret
\operatorname{D-mod}(K/K)
as the horizontal trace
of
\operatorname{D-mod}(K)
. Indeed, for any monoidal dg-category
\mathscr{A}
, the latter is
defined as

\mathscr{A}\underset{\mathscr{A}\otimes\mathscr{A}^{\operatorname{op}}}{\otimes}\mathscr{A}

or,
equivalently, as
the 2-categorical trace of
\mathscr{A}\operatorname{-mod}
, viewed as a dualizable
\operatorname{DGCat}_{\operatorname{cont}}
-linear
category. If we were working one categorical level lower, i.e. with an algebra
A
instead of a monoidal dg-category, the analogue of this discussion is the presentation of the Hochschild homology of
A\operatorname{-mod}
as

A\underset{A\otimes A^{\operatorname{op}}}{\otimes}A.

In the present case, using the correspondence between categorical coinvariants
and D-modules on quotient stacks, we
have a tautological identification

\operatorname{D-mod}(K)\underset{\operatorname{D-mod}(K\times K)}{\otimes}\operatorname{D-mod}(K)\simeq\operatorname{D-mod}(K\overset{K\times K}{\times}K)\simeq\operatorname{D-mod}(K/K).

General formalism assigns to a dualizable
\mathscr{A}
-module an
object of its horizontal trace, which recovers the preceding discussion of
character sheaves as a
special case; see
[
GKRV21
]
and
[
BZN09
]
for further discussion.

Remark 4.4.15
.

There are parallel theories to those discussed in this section, where
one works with analytic or étale constructible sheaves on
K
rather than
D-modules, cf. Section
6.10
below. When one takes étale sheaves on
K
defined over a finite field

\mathbb{F}_{q}
, a version of the functions-sheaves correspondence
identifies the category of modules for
K(\mathbb{F}_{q})
with the trace of Frobenius of

\operatorname{Shv}(K)\operatorname{-mod}
. This sends categorical representations to usual
representations and, compatibly, character sheaves to usual
characters; see
[
Gai16
]
.

4.5.
Complements II: generation

4.5.1.

Our final topic is several nice
theorems about generation. Recall a basic and useful feature of life is Morita
theory – for a given ring
A
, while its category of modules
A\operatorname{-mod}
is
compactly generated by
A
itself, it admits many other compact generators
c
. For any choice of one, taking its endomorphisms yields an equivalence

\operatorname{mod-}\operatorname{End}(c)\simeq A\operatorname{-mod},\quad\quad M\mapsto M\underset{\operatorname{End}(c)}{\otimes}c.

Said somewhat differently, a given dg-category
\mathscr{C}
may have many compact
generators and correspondingly many presentations as a category of modules. We
would like to review here a couple useful statements of this form, which
necessarily take place one categorical level higher, for

\operatorname{D-mod}(K)\operatorname{-mod}
.

4.5.2.

First of all, a nontrivial theorem of Beraldo
[
Ber17b
]
, building on work of Gaitsgory
[
Gai15
]
, states
that
\operatorname{D-mod}(K)
is generated by
\mathfrak{k}\operatorname{-mod}
. Note that the endomorphisms of the
latter are

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{D-mod}(K)\operatorname{-mod}}(\mathfrak{k}\operatorname{-mod},\mathfrak{k}\operatorname{-mod})

\displaystyle\simeq\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{DGCat}_{\operatorname{cont}}}(\mathfrak{k}\operatorname{-mod},\mathfrak{k}\operatorname{-mod})^{K}

\displaystyle\simeq(\mathfrak{k}\operatorname{-mod}^{\vee}\otimes\hskip 1.99168pt\mathfrak{k}\operatorname{-mod})^{K}

\displaystyle\simeq(\mathfrak{k}\operatorname{-mod}\otimes\hskip 1.99168pt\mathfrak{k}\operatorname{-mod})^{K}

\displaystyle\simeq(\mathfrak{k}\oplus\mathfrak{k}\operatorname{-mod})^{K}.

Stated in words, the equivariant endomorphisms of
\mathfrak{k}\operatorname{-mod}
are the category of Harish-Chandra
bimodules, i.e.,
\mathfrak{k}
-bimodules for which
the diagonal action is integrated to the group
K
.

Remark 4.5.3
.

We emphasize that this identity is an attractive and useful feature
of the
formalism. Namely, Harish-Chandra
bimodules have been studied and used in representation theory for many decades
and have been understood to have many favorable properties; see
[
BG80
]
and references therein. The above
articulates this in a precise sense – they are exactly the endofunctors
of
\mathfrak{k}\operatorname{-mod}
which commute with the categorical
K
-action and vice versa.

The theorem of Beraldo therefore affords an equivalence

\operatorname{D-mod}(K)\operatorname{-mod}\simeq(\mathfrak{k}\oplus\mathfrak{k}\operatorname{-mod}^{K})\operatorname{-mod}.

In particular, for any
\operatorname{D-mod}(K)
representation
\mathscr{C}
we have a
canonical equivalence

\mathfrak{k}\operatorname{-mod}\underset{\mathfrak{k}\oplus\mathfrak{k}\operatorname{-mod}^{K}}{\otimes}\mathscr{C}^{K,w}\simeq\mathscr{C}.

That is, recalling the equivalence
\operatorname{D-mod}(K)^{K,w}\simeq\mathfrak{k}\operatorname{-mod}
, a

\operatorname{D-mod}(K)
representation can be reconstructed from its weak invariants.

4.5.4.

We would also like to mention, for a reductive group
G
, a
nice analogue of highest weight theory in the present setting due to
Ben-Zvi–Gunningham–Orem
[
BZGO18
]
. Namely, they showed that, for any maximal unipotent subgroup
N
, an arbitrary
\operatorname{D-mod}(G)
representation
\mathscr{C}
can be reconstructed from its

N
-invariants, or even the weak Cartan
invariants of the latter

\mathscr{C}^{N}\quad\text{and}\quad(\mathscr{C}^{N})^{T,w}.

Passing to their endomorphisms, which roughly are Hecke categories without
prescribed monodromy along
T
-orbits, we obtain equivalences

\operatorname{D-mod}(G)\operatorname{-mod}\simeq\operatorname{D-mod}(N\backslash G/N)\operatorname{-mod}\simeq\operatorname{D-mod}(N\backslash G/N)^{T\times T,w}\operatorname{-mod}

and, in particular, identities

\operatorname{D-mod}(G/N)\underset{\operatorname{D-mod}(N\backslash G/N)}{\otimes}\mathscr{C}^{N}\simeq\operatorname{D-mod}(G/N)^{T,w}\underset{\operatorname{D-mod}(N\backslash G/N)^{T\times T,w}}{\otimes}(\mathscr{C}^{N})^{T,w}\simeq\mathscr{C}.

Remark 4.5.5
.

The argument of
[
BZGO18
]
yields the following useful variation. Given any
subgroup
H
of a
group
K
such that the quotient of
K
by the normalizer of
H
is proper,
the tautological map

\operatorname{D-mod}(K/H)\underset{\operatorname{D-mod}(H\backslash K/H)}{\otimes}\mathscr{C}^{K}\rightarrow\mathscr{C},

which need not be essentially surjective, is always a fully faithful
embedding. This may be applied to check equivalences
of categories on ‘highest weight vectors,’ which are often much easier to
directly analyze, cf.
[
CD21
]
,
[
Yan21
]
for further discussion and examples.

5.
Categorical representations of loop groups

5.1.
Overview

5.1.1.

So far, we have discussed Beilinson–Bernstein
localization and categorical representation theory for reductive groups.

In the remainder of this survey, we would like to turn to their affine
analogues, i.e.,
we pass from reductive groups to loop groups. The story here is
significantly richer and is the subject of the local geometric Langlands
correspondence.

In this section, we will give some introductory comments on loop groups and
their categorical representations to prepare for a discussion of the
Langlands correspondence in Section
6
.

5.2.
Loop groups

5.2.1.

As before, we continue to denote by
G
a connected
reductive group over an algebraically closed field
k
of characteristic
zero.

The main actor in this section is not
G
, but rather its loop group
LG
. At first
pass, just
as
G
consists of certain matrices with entries in
k
, i.e., pick a
faithful representation,
LG
consists of the same form of matrices but
with entries in the field of Laurent series
k(\!(t)\!)
. Somewhat more
carefully, since we need to make sense of
LG
as an algebro-geometric
object, it is a certain group ind-scheme whose closed points are as we just
described.

Example 5.2.2
.

Here is an explicit construction in the case when

G
is the
(non-reductive) additive group
\mathbb{G}_{a}
, which contains most of the
representative ideas. In this case, we have

\mathbb{G}_{a}(k)\simeq k
, i.e., it is a one dimensional affine space. As above, we
would like
L\mathbb{G}_{a}(k)\simeq k(\!(t)\!)
. The basic idea is that

k(\!(t)\!)
is again an infinite dimensional affine space, and, in fact, by
bounding the order of the pole of the Laurent series, an ascending union of
pro-finite dimensional affine spaces. Explicitly as a vector space

k(\!(t)\!)=\varinjlim_{n}\varprojlim_{m}t^{-n}\cdot k[[t]]/t^{-n+m}\cdot k[[t]],

and we use the same formula to describe its structure as an ind-scheme.
That is, fixing
n
, we obtain a pro-finite dimensional affine space, which
explicitly is the spectrum of a polynomial ring in countably many variables, i.e., the functions corresponding to the Taylor coefficients of a series.
Allowing
n
to vary, we obtain an ascending union of infinite dimensional
affine spaces along closed embeddings, i.e., an ind-scheme.

Remark 5.2.3
.

In general,
LG
admits a succinct description via its functor
of points, which is defined as
follows. For any variety
X
, consider the functor
LX
from affine
varieties to sets whose

R
-points, for any
k
-algebra
R
, are

LX(R)=X(R(\!(t)\!)).

While this is not well-behaved for a general variety, if
X
is affine, this
is representable by an ind-scheme of ind-infinite type. Taking
X=G

yields the loop group
LG
.

Having given a formal definition, let us try to give a slightly more hands-on description in the spirit of Example
5.2.2
. Recall the analogy
between
G(\mathbb{F}_{q})
and
G(k)
and, more
generally, finite
G(\mathbb{F}_{q})
-sets and finite dimensional
G(k)
-varieties as
discussed in the previous sections. A basic guiding principle for the
present situation is an
analogy between the
p
-adic group

G(\mathbb{F}_{q}(\!(t)\!))
and the loop group
LG
.

Remark 5.2.4
.

While some authors use the term
p
-adic group to mean the points of
G
in
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
or a finite extension thereof, here by a mild abuse of notation we call the points in any non-Archimedean local field, such as
\mathbb{F}_{q}(\!(t)\!)
, a
p
-adic group.

Recall that
p
-adic groups, with their natural topology, are ind-pro-finite
sets. Explicitly, fix any compact open subgroup
K
, i.e., a subgroup such that
K\cap G(\mathbb{F}_{q}[[t]])
is finite index in both
K
and
G(\mathbb{F}_{q}[[t]])
. Then
K
is
naturally an inverse limit of finite groups, and the entire group decomposes as
a disjoint union of its
K
cosets. Concretely, for
G(\mathbb{F}_{q}[[t]])
, the
relevant inverse limit comes from the topology on power series, i.e.,

(5.1)

G(\mathbb{F}_{q}[[t]])=\varprojlim_{n}G(\mathbb{F}_{q}[t]/(t^{n})).

In a similar way, one can make sense of compact open subgroups of
LG
. Namely,
one has the arc group
L^{+}G
, which is a group scheme of infinite type with

k
-points
G(k[[t]])
. This is defined as an inverse limit of finite dimensional
algebraic groups similarly to (
5.1
). A sub-group scheme
K

of
LG
is said to be compact open if
K\cap L^{+}G
is of finite
codimension in both
K
and
L^{+}G
. Then
K
is naturally an inverse limit of algebraic
groups, and the coset space

LG/K

is an ind-scheme of ind-finite type. We
emphasize that, under the analogy between finite sets
and finite dimensional varieties, this corresponds to being a countable discrete union
of
cosets in the case of
p
-adic groups.

5.3.
D-modules on loop groups and categorical actions

5.3.1.

We would next like to discuss the convolution algebra of
D-modules on
LG
. Since this is an infinite-dimensional object, some care
is required.

Its definition may be arrived at as follows. Recall that we thought of
modules for
\operatorname{D-mod}(G)
as akin to representations of
G(\mathbb{F}_{q})
. In
particular, the convolution algebra of D-modules itself was analogous to
the group algebra
\operatorname{Fun}(G(\mathbb{F}_{q}))
.

We would similarly like modules for
\operatorname{D-mod}(LG)
to
behave like representations of the
p
-adic group
G(\mathbb{F}_{q}(\!(t)\!))
. As the latter
is a topological group, one correspondingly works with smooth
representations.
11
11

11

Explicitly, a representation on a complex vector space
V
is smooth if the action map
G(\mathbb{F}_{q}(\!(t)\!))\times V\rightarrow V
is continuous with respect to the discrete
topology on
V
, i.e., the stabilizer of each vector is open. For the reader
unfamiliar with
p
-adic representation theory, the point here is that this condition mirrors a similar one for Lie
group representations, where one asks for the action to be suitably smooth in the sense of manifolds. In the
p
-adic setting, however, the
topologies on the group and on the vector space are,
informally speaking, ‘coprime.’ Practically, smooth representations are what show up in
nature when studying automorphic forms.
For this reason, modules for
G(\mathbb{F}_{q}(\!(t)\!))
are not the same as modules for its group algebra, but instead for
its Hecke algebra

\operatorname{Dist}(G(\mathbb{F}_{q}(\!(t)\!)))

of compactly supported distributions. Concretely, this can be written as
the
colimit

(5.2)

\operatorname{Dist}(G(\mathbb{F}_{q}(\!(t)\!)))\simeq\varinjlim_{K}\operatorname{Dist}(K\backslash G(\mathbb{F}_{q}(\!(t)\!))/K),

where
K
runs over compact open subgroups and each
\operatorname{Dist}(K\backslash G(\mathbb{F}_{q}(\!(t)\!))/K)
is simply finite linear combinations of delta distributions on
double cosets, i.e., distributions that integrate a function over a double
coset
KgK
with respect to some Haar measure.

With this in mind, the category of D-modules on the loop group is defined
as

\operatorname{D-mod}(LG)\simeq\varinjlim\operatorname{D-mod}(K\backslash LG/K),

where
K
runs over compact open subgroups and the colimit is taken in

\operatorname{DGCat}_{\operatorname{cont}}
along
*
-pullback functors. It remains then to describe, for
a fixed
K
, the category

\operatorname{D-mod}(K\backslash LG/K)
. However, recalling that
LG/K
is an ind-scheme
of ind-finite type, we may moreover write it as an ascending union

LG/K=\bigcup_{i}X_{i},

where each
X_{i}
is a closed subscheme of finite type that is moreover
K

invariant. As
K
acts on each
X_{i}
via a finite dimensional quotient
Q_{i}

with prounipotent kernel, we have

\operatorname{D-mod}(X_{i})^{K}\simeq\operatorname{D-mod}(X_{i})^{Q_{i}}\simeq\operatorname{D-mod}(Q_{i}\backslash X_{i}),

i.e., we are dealing with D-modules on a usual finite dimensional stack. We then
set

\operatorname{D-mod}(K\backslash LG/K)\simeq\varinjlim\operatorname{D-mod}(Q_{i}\backslash X_{i}).

The point here is that, roughly speaking, objects in

\operatorname{D-mod}(LG)
resemble compactly supported distributions on
G(\mathbb{F}_{q}(\!(t)\!))
. More
precisely, by Example
4.2.12
,
\operatorname{D-mod}(LG)
is compactly
generated, with compact objects given by
*
-extensions of bounded
complexes of coherent D-modules from subvarieties of
LG/K
, for some
K
.

While the exact details of this construction are not so important for us, here are a couple orienting remarks.

Remark 5.3.2
.

First, one can replace all possible
K
in the above colimit (
5.2
) with
any cofinal subset. A concrete such choice would be the congruence
subgroups
K_{n}
, for
n\geqslant 1
, of
L^{+}G
, which we will simply
describe at the level of

k
-points via the exact sequence

1\rightarrow K_{n}\rightarrow L^{+}G=G(k[[t]])\rightarrow G(k[[t]]/(t^{n}))\rightarrow 1.

Noting that
K_{1}
is the prounipotent radical of
L^{+}G
, it follows that
any sufficiently small compact open subgroup
K
is
prounipotent. In particular, for two such prounipotent subgroups
K^{\prime}\subset K
, the
pullback

\operatorname{D-mod}(K\backslash LG/K)\rightarrow\operatorname{D-mod}(K^{\prime}\backslash LG/K^{\prime})

is a fully faithful embedding, cf. Remark
4.3.8
.

Remark 5.3.3
.

While the above construction may seem somewhat ad hoc, we should
mention that it is a special case of a similar definition of D-modules for
a larger
class of infinite dimensional varieties known as placid ind-schemes
[
Ras15a
]
.

In fact, a basic subtlety in this infinite dimensional setting is that there are two dual versions of the category of D-modules. One comes with functoriality for
*
-pushforwards and the other with functoriality for
!
-pullbacks. The presentation above is for the category with
*
-pushforwards. This is the analogue of the distinction between functions and distributions on
G(\mathbb{F}_{q}(\!(t)\!))
. Relatedly, the dual categories for
LG
may be identified after trivializing the dimension torsor, i.e., fixing a compact open subgroup.

5.3.4.

As in the finite dimensional case,
\operatorname{D-mod}(LG)
is a
monoidal dg-category under convolution. In particular, as before, we can
define its category of modules

\operatorname{D-mod}(LG)\operatorname{-mod}.

Here are some basic examples of representations.

Example 5.3.5
.

If
X
is a placid ind-scheme acted on by
LG
, its category of
D-modules
\operatorname{D-mod}(X)
carries a canonical action of
\operatorname{D-mod}(LG)
by
convolution.

Example 5.3.6
.

Given a central extension of the loop group

1\rightarrow\mathbb{G}_{m}\rightarrow\widetilde{LG}\rightarrow LG\rightarrow 1,

one obtains upon passing to Lie algebras a central extension of the loop algebra, i.e., an affine Lie
algebra

0\rightarrow k\cdot 1\rightarrow\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}\rightarrow L\mathfrak{g}\rightarrow 0.

There is a canonical action of
\operatorname{D-mod}(LG)
on the derived category of
smooth modules

\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}\operatorname{-mod},

induced by the fact that the adjoint action of
\widetilde{LG}
on
\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}
factors through
LG
. By definition, an object of the corresponding abelian category is a
representation of
\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}
on which 1 acts by the identity and where each vector
is annihilated by a compact open subalgebra. The full dg-category,
following Frenkel–Gaitsgory
[
FG09a
]
, is a slight
modification of the naive unbounded derived category, such that objects
like Verma modules or, more generally, inductions of finite dimensional
representations of compact open subalgebras are compact generators.

While
the details of this will not be important for us, let us simply remark
that the existence and utility of this modification stem from the fact that the abelian category
is of infinite cohomological dimension. Relatedly, the renormalized derived
category carries a
t
-structure and canonically identifies on bounded
below parts with
the usual derived category. That is, the renormalization leads only to
differences in
cohomological degree

-\infty
. Similarly, the category

\operatorname{D-mod}(LG)
carries a
t
-structure, which is canonical up to an overall
shift,
12
12

12

This mirrors the uniqueness of the Haar measure on

G(\mathbb{F}_{q}(\!(t)\!))
up to scaling.
with the property that
\operatorname{D-mod}(LG)
agrees with the naive unbounded
derived
category of its heart, modulo issues in cohomological degree

-\infty
.

6.
Local geometric Langlands and affine Beilinson–Bernstein localization

6.1.
Overview

6.1.1.

In the previous sections, we discussed localization theory for reductive Lie algebras and explained its relation to the categorical representation theory of reductive groups. In Section
5
, we met the loop groups and their categorical representations.

In this final section, we will sketch some of
the basic conjectures and results in local
geometric Langlands, which may be understood as parametrizing the categorical representations of loop groups. At the end, we will come full circle and meet an
analogue
of Beilinson–Bernstein localization for loop groups, as developed by
Beilinson–Drinfeld, Frenkel–Gaitsgory, and Raskin–Yang.

6.2.
A naive formulation of the correspondence

6.2.1.

For the
p
-adic group
G(\mathbb{F}_{q}(\!(t)\!))
, the local Langlands
correspondence (conjecturally) parametrizes its irreducible representations
on
k
vector spaces in terms of Galois theoretic data, to first
approximation homomorphisms
from the absolute
Galois group of
\mathbb{F}_{q}(\!(t)\!)
into the Langlands dual
\check{G}(k)
.

In this subsection we would like to review what is expected and what is
known about an analogous parametrization of
\operatorname{D-mod}(LG)\operatorname{-mod}
.

6.2.2.

Recall that
k(\!(t)\!)
consists of functions on a formal
punctured disk
D^{\times}
. Following the analogy between Galois
representations and local systems, cf. Appendix
A
, the counterparts to
\check{G}
-valued Galois
representations in the present setting are flat
\check{G}
connections, i.e.,
\check{G}
de Rham local
systems
on

D^{\times}
.

Concretely, these are given by connections modulo gauge
equivalence, i.e.,

\operatorname{LocSys}_{\check{G}}\simeq\{d+\check{\mathfrak{g}}(\!(t)\!)dt\}/\check{G}(k(\!(t)\!)).

The same formula defines
\operatorname{LocSys}_{\check{G}}
further as an object of algebraic geometry,
i.e., one takes the quotient prestack of the action of the
L\check{G}
on the
ind-scheme of connection forms
d+\check{\mathfrak{g}}(\!(t)\!)dt
, which is defined
similarly to Example
5.2.2
; see
[
Ras15b
]
for further discussion.

6.2.3.

Let us try to arrive at the statement of the local
geometric Langlands
correspondence in a somewhat heuristic fashion. To first approximation, we
can hope that irreducible
\operatorname{D-mod}(LG)
representations are in
bijection with the points of
\operatorname{LocSys}_{\check{G}}
. That is, to each
\check{G}
-connection

\sigma
one should be able to canonically attach an irreducible categorical
representation
\mathscr{C}_{\sigma}
of
LG
.

Because a general object
\mathscr{C}
of
\operatorname{D-mod}(LG)\operatorname{-mod}
should decompose as a direct
integral of irreducible representations, it should define a corresponding
family of categories over
\operatorname{LocSys}_{\check{G}}
, whose fibres over each point measure the
corresponding multiplicity of
\mathscr{C}_{\sigma}
in the direct integral.

Since irreducibility is a feature of objects of abelian categories,
this discussion is difficult to formulate precisely. However, there is a
good notion of a family of categories over a space, namely a
quasicoherent sheaf of categories, which we review below.

Modulo corrections, which we will meet duly in Equation (
6.19
) below, this gives the
statement of the local geometric Langlands correspondence. Namely, the conjecture is
that there should be an equivalence of
(\infty,2)
-categories between all
categorical representations of the loop group and sheaves of categories on
the moduli space of local systems, i.e.,

(6.1)

\mathbb{L}:\operatorname{D-mod}(LG)\operatorname{-mod}\simeq\operatorname{2-QCoh}(\operatorname{LocSys}_{\check{G}}).

Here is a basic orienting remark. The asserted equivalence of 2-categories is not saying that for a given categorical representation
\mathscr{C}
, the underlying
dg-categories of
\mathscr{C}
and
\mathbb{L}(\mathscr{C})
are equivalent. Rather, it is saying that, for any pair of objects
\mathscr{C}
and
\mathscr{D}
, there is an
equivalence

\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{D-mod}(LG)\operatorname{-mod}}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D})\simeq\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{2-QCoh}(\operatorname{LocSys}_{\check{G}})}(\mathbb{L}(\mathscr{C}),\mathbb{L}(\mathscr{D})),

and these are compatible with composition as one varies
\mathscr{C}
and
\mathscr{D}
.

Remark 6.2.4
.

As a toy model, a Morita equivalence for usual algebras

A\operatorname{-mod}\simeq B\operatorname{-mod}

matches not the underlying vector spaces of
modules, but
only their homomorphisms. Explicitly, one can consider, for a finite
dimensional
k
vector space
V
, the Morita equivalence

V\otimes-:\operatorname{Vect}\simeq\operatorname{End}(V)\operatorname{-mod}.

This exchanges
k
and
V
, which certainly are not isomorphic, but on
endomorphisms we do have

\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{Vect}}(k,k)\simeq k\simeq\operatorname{Hom}_{\operatorname{End}(V)}(V,V).

Before turning to corrections, we would like to first discuss
sheaves of categories and some of the basic compatibilites
\mathbb{L}

is
expected to satisfy.

6.2.5.

To discuss sheaves of categories, let us start with the
case of an affine variety
X=\operatorname{Spec}O_{X}
. In this case, a
quasi-coherent
sheaf of categories on
X
is simply an
O_{X}
-linear, as opposed to merely

k
-linear, dg-category. Since
O_{X}
-linearity is the same as being able to
tensor by complexes of
O_{X}
-modules, we have equivalently that

\operatorname{2-QCoh}(X)\simeq\operatorname{QCoh}(X)\operatorname{-mod}.

In particular
\operatorname{2-QCoh}(\operatorname{pt})
is simply
\operatorname{DGCat}_{\operatorname{cont}}
, and, for any closed
point
\operatorname{pt}\rightarrow X
, one has a pullback

\operatorname{Vect}\underset{\operatorname{QCoh}(X)}{\otimes}-:\operatorname{2-QCoh}(X)\rightarrow\operatorname{DGCat}_{\operatorname{cont}},

i.e., one can speak of the fibres of a sheaf of categories, which are usual
dg-categories. In this way, one thinks of a sheaf of categories as a
family of categories over
X
.

Remark 6.2.6
.

More generally, given any map
f:X\rightarrow Y
pullback
defines a monoidal functor
\operatorname{QCoh}(Y)\rightarrow\operatorname{QCoh}(X)
, and, in
particular, adjoint induction and restriction functors

f^{*}:\operatorname{2-QCoh}(Y)\leftrightarrows\operatorname{2-QCoh}(X):f_{*}.

Here is a basic example of a sheaf of categories.

Example 6.2.7
.

Given a map
\tilde{X}\rightarrow X
of varieties,

\operatorname{QCoh}(\tilde{X})
is a
\operatorname{QCoh}(X)
-algebra and, in particular, a sheaf of categories over
X
. For any
point
x
of
X
, the fibre of the sheaf of categories is, by Example

4.2.25
, simply
the quasicoherent sheaves on the actual fibre, i.e.,

i_{x}^{*}\operatorname{QCoh}(\tilde{X})\simeq\operatorname{QCoh}(\tilde{X}\underset{X}{\times}x).

6.2.8.

For a general
X
, not necessarily affine, the definition
proceeds by gluing,
exactly as in the definition of quasicoherent sheaves. While the details are not so important for us, we should mention the
following beautiful phenomenon established by Gaitsgory in
[
Gai15
]
. Namely, for general
X
, one by definition produces sheaves of categories by
patching them affine by affine. On the other hand, one also has the global
monoidal category of quasicoherent sheaves and hence its category of left
modules
\operatorname{QCoh}(X)\operatorname{-mod}
. One can therefore ask for the relation between the two. As for usual
quasicoherent sheaves, one
has a
global sections functor

(6.2)

\Gamma:\operatorname{2-QCoh}(X)\rightarrow\operatorname{QCoh}(X)\operatorname{-mod}.

For usual sheaves, the analogous map

\Gamma:\operatorname{QCoh}(X)\rightarrow\Gamma(X,\mathscr{O}_{X})\operatorname{-mod}

is rarely an equivalence if
X
is not affine.
13
13

13

For the reader
wondering about the use of ‘rarely’, the point is that when one considers
the full
derived algebra of global functions,
\Gamma
can still be an isomorphism
for non-affine varieties, e.g., for open subvarieties of affine varieties.

By contrast, for most finite dimensional
X
, e.g., any algebraic stack of
finite type, the functor (
6.2
) is an equivalence, a
phenomenon called 1-affineness.

A basic conjecture in the subject, due to Gaitsgory, is that
\operatorname{LocSys}_{\check{G}}
is also
1-affine; see
[
Ras15b
]
. This is a subtle assertion, as 1-affineness does not hold in
general for ind-schemes or classifying stacks of group schemes of infinite
type. So, in some sense, one needs the two types of issues to ‘cancel
out’ for
\operatorname{LocSys}_{\check{G}}
. This was partially addressed in an
important work of Raskin
[
Ras15b
]
, which
showed that
(
6.2
) is fully faithful for
X=\operatorname{LocSys}_{\check{G}}
.

6.3.
Properties of the correspondence I

6.3.1.

Having dispensed with a discussion of definitions,
let us
get to the heart of the matter and discuss what
\mathbb{L}
is expected to
look like. More precisely, let us list a few basic objects on each side
that are expected to be exchanged with one another. Combined with a
compatibility with parabolic induction, this is expected to largely pin
down the equivalence.

The structure of our discussion will be as follows. We have listed the categories exchanged in Figure
1
. For each row in the figure, we will spend a little
time describing the objects that appear on either side and motivate why one
should expect them to be
exchanged. Finally, we will see what matching intertwining operators
amounts to in these cases and, in particular, we will meet the promised
correction of (
6.1
).

Categorical representations of
LG

Sheaves of categories on
\operatorname{LocSys}_{\check{G}}

\hskip 0.0pt\operatorname{D-mod}(\operatorname{Gr_{G}})

\operatorname{QCoh}(\operatorname{pt}/\check{G})

\hskip 0.0pt\operatorname{D-mod}(\operatorname{Fl}^{\operatorname{aff}}_{G})

\hskip 0.0pt\operatorname{QCoh}(\widetilde{{\mathscr{N}}}/\check{G})

\hskip 0.0pt\operatorname{D-mod}(LG/LN,\psi)

\hskip 0.0pt\operatorname{QCoh}(\operatorname{LocSys}_{\check{G}})

\hskip 0.0pt\widehat{\mathfrak{g}}_{\kappa_{c}}\operatorname{-mod}

\hskip 0.0pt\operatorname{QCoh}(\mathrm{Op}_{\check{G}})

Figure 1.
Some objects (conjecturally) exchanged under local
geometric Langlands.

6.3.2.

For the first two examples from Figure
1
, it is
clarifying to recall some
basic features of
p
-adic representation theory. By smoothness, any
representation
\pi
of a
p
-adic group contains nontrivial invariants
\pi^{K}

for all sufficiently small compact open subgroups
K
. For such a
K
, if
\pi
is
irreducible, the fixed vectors
\pi^{K}
will be an irreducible
representation of the Hecke algebra
\operatorname{Dist}(K\backslash G(\mathbb{F}_{q}(\!(t)\!))/K)
,
which naturally acts on it by convolution. In this way, one obtains a bijection
between irreducible representations of

G(\mathbb{F}_{q}(\!(t)\!))
containing
K
-invariant vectors and irreducible representations of
this Hecke algebra.

As one shrinks the subgroup
K
, the corresponding Hecke algebra grows, and
this gives a
basic measure of the complexity of a representation. Namely, for a given
irreducible
\pi
, one can ask for a maximal
K
for which one has nontrivial
invariants, and, in some sense, the larger
K
is, the less complicated
\pi
is.

On the other side of local Langlands, the (Weil–Deligne variant of the) absolute
Galois group of
\mathbb{F}_{q}(\!(t)\!)
carries a canonical filtration by descending normal
subgroups called the ramification filtration. For a given
\check{G}(k)
-valued Galois
representation, one can measure its complexity by asking through what quotient
of this filtration the action factors. A basic and striking organizing principle is that
these two measures of complexity are exchanged under local Langlands.
14
14

14

More precisely, the depth of a representation, which is a certain nonnegative rational number defined by Moy–Prasad
[
MP94
]
, should coincide with the slope of the corresponding Galois representation, as measured by the upper numbering ramification filtration.

6.4.
Properties of the correspondence II: unramified representations

6.4.1.

From the preceding discussion,
it follows that the most easily understood representations of
p
-adic
groups should be those that have fixed vectors with respect to the maximal
compact open subgroup
G(\mathbb{F}_{q}[[t]])
.
15
15

15

We should note that, for
groups
other
than
GL_{n}
, there
are other non-conjugate maximal compact subgroups.
Similarly, the simplest
Galois representations should be those on which the action factors through
the final quotient of the ramification filtration, i.e., they extend to

\check{G}
-local systems on the non-punctured disk. Explicitly, these
are precisely the representations which factor through the Galois group of
the residue field

(6.3)

1\rightarrow I\rightarrow\operatorname{Gal}(\mathbb{F}_{q}(\!(t)\!)^{s}/\mathbb{F}_{q}(\!(t)\!))\rightarrow\operatorname{Gal}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{q}/\mathbb{F}_{q})\simeq\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}\rightarrow 1,

where the superscript ‘
s
’ denotes the separable closure.

Since one actually works with the Weil group, which by definition replaces

\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}
with
\mathbb{Z}
, unramified Galois
representations, up to isomorphism, are
specified by elements of
\check{G}
, up to conjugacy. That is, we follow the image
of
1\in\mathbb{Z}
under a map
\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\check{G}
. In this simplest case, local
Langlands reduces to the Satake isomorphism between the spherical Hecke
algebra and the character ring of
\check{G}
, i.e.,

\operatorname{Dist}(G(\mathbb{F}_{q}[[t]])\backslash G(\mathbb{F}_{q}(\!(t)\!))/G(\mathbb{F}_{q}[[t]]))\simeq\operatorname{Fun}(\check{G}(k)/\check{G}(k)),

where the right hand side denotes the adjoint invariant polynomial
functions on
\check{G}
, viewed as a variety over
\operatorname{Spec}k

[
Sat63
]
. By passing to
their characters, we obtain that
irreducible unramified
representations of
G(\mathbb{F}_{q}(\!(t)\!))
are
parametrized by (semisimple) conjugacy classes in
\check{G}(k)
, as desired.

Remark 6.4.2
.

For
the reader
encountering these ideas for the first time, we emphasize that even the
commutativity of the appearing Hecke algebra is not a priori obvious. This is illustrative of how passing to bi-invariant distributions for a large
compact subgroup dramatically simplifies life.

6.4.3.

To prepare for the geometric version, we should mention that each
irreducible unramified
representation embeds, uniquely up to scaling, into the universal
unramified representation

(6.4)

\operatorname{Fun}(G(\mathbb{F}_{q}(\!(t)\!))/G(\mathbb{F}_{q}[[t]])).

Moreover, to extract
each from the universal case, one takes the submodule of (
6.4
)
which transforms by the corresponding character under right convolution by
the spherical Hecke algebra.

Finally, let us discuss the analogues of the above in local geometric
Langlands. On the automorphic side, we consider the category of
D-modules on the affine Grassmannian

\operatorname{D-mod}(\operatorname{Gr}_{G})=\operatorname{D-mod}(LG/L^{+}G)

as a substitute in a way that is hopefully familiar by now to the
above universal
unramified representation. On the spectral side, any de Rham local system
on
the punctured disk that extends to the full disk is trivializable, i.e., we
obtain the stacky point

\operatorname{pt}/\check{G}\rightarrow\operatorname{LocSys}_{\check{G}}

and correspondingly view
\operatorname{QCoh}(\operatorname{pt}/\check{G})
as a sheaf of categories
over
\operatorname{LocSys}_{\check{G}}
.

We should also mention that one has analogues of not only the universal
case, but also of an individual irreducible unramified representation. We defer
a more detailed discussion until Section
6.9
.

Remark 6.4.4
.

We should note that, while the unramified case is the most basic,
it is completely fundamental in both arithmetic and geometric Langlands.
Namely, in the global Langlands correspondence, which we do not discuss in
detail here, any irreducible smooth representation of the adelic group

G(\mathbb{A}_{F})
factorizes as a restricted tensor product of irreducible
representations of the
p
-adic groups
G(F_{x})
at each place, and
all but finitely many are unramified. To even make sense of such an infinite tensor product, one makes essential use of the fact that unramified representations have canonical generating lines, namely their spherical invariants. In the global geometric theory, almost
all that is known so far is for the analogue of everywhere unramified (or
possibly tamely ramified) automorphic representations.

6.5.
Properties of the correspondence III: unramified principal
series

6.5.1.

Given the unramified
representations of
p
-adic groups, one can produce further representations
as follows. As in other parts of representation theory, one has
parabolic induction functors that allow one to build representations of
G

starting with representations of smaller groups, such as the Cartan
T
.
Feeding unramified representations into this construction yields an
important family of modules, the unramified principal series. Their simple subquotients are precisely the simple
G(\mathbb{F}_{q}(\!(t)\!))
-modules with nontrivial
invariants for the compact open Iwahori subgroup
I
, i.e., the preimage of
the Borel of
B(\mathbb{F}_{q})
of
G(\mathbb{F}_{q})
under the projection

G(\mathbb{F}_{q}[[t]])\rightarrow G(\mathbb{F}_{q}).

That is, applying parabolic induction to unramified
representations yields the next simplest family of representations of

p
-adic groups.

In spectral terms, such irreducible representations of
G(\mathbb{F}_{q}(\!(t)\!))
correspond to Galois representations
that are tamely ramified with unipotent
monodromy. To explain this, recall from the sequence (
6.3
) the inertia subgroup

I\simeq\operatorname{Gal}(\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{q}(\!(t)\!)^{s}/\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{q}(\!(t)\!)),

where
\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
denotes an algebraic closure of
\mathbb{F}_{q}
. The next quotient in the ramification filtration splits this as

1\rightarrow I_{\operatorname{wild}}\rightarrow I\rightarrow I_{\operatorname{tame}}\rightarrow 1.

The basic idea for
I_{\operatorname{tame}}
, the tame inertia, is that it behaves like its characteristic zero counterpart, i.e., covers of a formal punctured disk over
k
. These covers are classified exactly like the covering spaces of a circle in topology, i.e., one adjoins an
n^{th}
root of
t
for a positive integer
n
. In positive characteristic, these are separable if and only if
n
is coprime to the characteristic of
q
, and such covers exhaust all Galois extensions of degree coprime to
q
. Explicitly, one may choose an isomorphism

I_{\operatorname{tame}}\simeq\underset{\ell}{\Pi}\hskip 1.99168pt\mathbb{Z}_{\ell},

where
\ell
runs over primes not equal to the characteristic of
\mathbb{F}_{q}
. A Galois representation is said to be tamely ramified if it factors through the quotient by
I_{\operatorname{wild}}
, and it is further said to be unipotently monodromic if any element of
I_{\operatorname{tame}}
, or equivalently a topological generator, is sent to a unipotent element of
\check{G}
.

6.5.2.

To orient ourselves for the geometric discussion, let us review in slightly more detail how the relevant parabolic induction of
p
-adic representations goes. Given the Borel

B
, whose Levi quotient is the abstract Cartan
T
, one obtains a correspondence on rational points

T(\mathbb{F}_{q}(\!(t)\!))\leftarrow B(\mathbb{F}_{q}(\!(t)\!))\rightarrow G(\mathbb{F}_{q}(\!(t)\!)),

where the leftward arrow is a surjection with kernel the rational points of
the unipotent radical
N(\mathbb{F}_{q}(\!(t)\!))
, and the rightward arrow is a closed
embedding. This gives rise to a functor of parabolic induction,

(6.5)

T(\mathbb{F}_{q}(\!(t)\!))\operatorname{-mod}\xrightarrow{\operatorname{Res}}B(\mathbb{F}_{q}(\!(t)\!))\operatorname{-mod}\xrightarrow{\operatorname{Ind}}G(\mathbb{F}_{q}(\!(t)\!))\operatorname{-mod}.

Explicitly, one inflates a smooth representation
\pi
of
T(\mathbb{F}_{q}(\!(t)\!))
to a
representation of
B(\mathbb{F}_{q}(\!(t)\!))
by having the unipotent radical act trivially,
and then one induces up to
G(\mathbb{F}_{q}(\!(t)\!))
. This induction, as usual, is concretely given
by smooth sections of the corresponding vector bundle over the flag
manifold, i.e.,

G(\mathbb{F}_{q}(\!(t)\!))\overset{B(\mathbb{F}_{q}(\!(t)\!))}{\times}\pi\rightarrow G(\mathbb{F}_{q}(\!(t)\!))/B(\mathbb{F}_{q}(\!(t)\!)).

In particular, it follows that any parabolic induction of an
unramified character of
T
embeds into the parabolic induction of the
universal unramified representation, i.e. the universal principal series module

\operatorname{Fun}\big(G(\mathbb{F}_{q}(\!(t)\!))/T(\mathbb{F}_{q}[[t]])\cdot N(\mathbb{F}_{q}(\!(t)\!))\big).

Remark 6.5.3
.

For a reader who has not explicitly encountered parabolic
induction before, it may be orienting to realize that the classification of
irreducible algebraic representations of reductive groups by their highest
weight is an example of parabolic induction.

Indeed, for the algebraic
groups
G,B,T
and
N
as above, one has adjunctions between their
categories of algebraic representations over any field

\operatorname{Rep}(G)\overset{\operatorname{Res}}{\underset{\operatorname{Ind}}{\rightleftarrows}}\operatorname{Rep}(B)\overset{\operatorname{Coinv}_{N}}{\underset{\operatorname{Res}}{\rightleftarrows}}\operatorname{Rep}(T),

where
\operatorname{Res}
denotes restriction,
\operatorname{Coinv}_{N}
denotes the functor of

N
coinvariants, and
\operatorname{Ind}
is the functor of coinduction.
Concatenating these, one obtains the functor of parabolic restriction,
which takes a
G
-module and returns its
N
-coinvariants, viewed as a

T
-module, as well as its right adjoint functor of parabolic coinduction,
which takes a
T
-module, extends it to a
B
-module with a trivial
action of
N
, and coinduces this to
G
.

With this, the theorem of
the highest weight, which can be phrased as each irreducible
G
-module
having a line of
N
-coinvariants, in particular implies that every
irreducible
G
-module appears as a submodule of a parabolically coinduced
module. Moreover, in characteristic zero, by complete reducibility it
follows that every irreducible coincides with a parabolically coinduced module. Via the
explicit model of the coinduction of a character as sections of a line bundle, this recovers
the
Borel–Weil theorem about sections of line bundles on
G/B
.

6.5.4.

Let us turn to the geometric case. In this case, the
automorphic side will
similarly be D-modules on a semi-infinite flag manifold, i.e.,

\operatorname{D-mod}(LG/L^{+}T\cdot LN),

which, as before, may be constructed from
\operatorname{D-mod}(\operatorname{Gr}_{T})
by a suitable
functor of parabolic induction similar to (
6.5
).

Remark 6.5.5
.

Here is a technical comment regarding this category. It is as
yet unknown how to treat the space
LG/L^{+}T\cdot LN
